115 年 8 月海關進出口貿易初步統計 
一、進出口貿易變動 
（一）出口 
8 月出口 824.0 億美元，創歷年單月新高，較上月增 9.4%，較上年
同月增 41.0%(按新臺幣計算增 52.6%)；累計 1 至 8 月出口 5,743.4
億美元，較上年同期增 44.2%(按新臺幣計算增 46.5%)。 
（二）進口 
8 月進口 601.0 億美元，為歷年單月第 3 高，較上月增 3.4%，較上
年同月增 44.3%(按新臺幣計算增 56.2%)；累 計1 至 8 月進口 4,374.3
億美元，較上年同期增 40.4%(按新臺幣計算增 42.4%)。 
（三）出入超 
8 月出超 223.0 億美元，為單月新高，較上年同月增 55.2 億美元；
累計 1 至 8 月出超 1,369.1 億美元，較上年同期增 500.5 億美元。

### Page 2

二、出口主要貨品 
（一）與上年同月比較 
受惠於人工智慧(AI)、 高效能運算及雲端服務等應用持續擴增，相關
產品出貨維持強勁， 加上價格攀升效 應，8月出口較上年同月增 239.7
億美元(+41.0%)，主要變動如下： 
1. 電子零組件出口 322.2 億美元(歷年單月新高)， 較上年同月增118.3
億美元(+58.0%)，其中積體電路增 115.5 億美元(+60.0%)、印刷電
路增 1.4 億美元(+29.9%)、 電容器及電阻器增0.7 億美元(+24.9%)。 
2. 資通與視聽產品出口 331.0 億美元，較上年同月增 97.6 億美元
(+41.8%)，其中電腦及其附屬單元增68.7 億美元(+40.4%)、儲存媒
體增 19.1 億美元(+2.4 倍)、 交換器及路由器增9.7 億美元(+1.1 倍)。 
3. 礦產品出口 20.0 億美元，較上年同月增 8.3 億美元(+70.2%)，其
中石油煉製品增 7.8 億美元(+74.3%)。 
4. 基本金屬及其製品出口 29.6 億美元，較上年同月增 5.4 億美元
(+22.4%)，其中銅及其製品增 6.1 億美元(+1.5 倍)，鋼鐵及其製品
減 1.1 億美元(-8.7%)。 
5. 機械出口 26.8 億美元，較上年同月增 3.6 億美元(+15.2%)，其中
生產半導體等機械增 1.9 億美元(+42.1%)、未列名具特殊功能機械
增 0.4 億美元(+39.0%)。

累計 1 至 8 月出口較上年同期增 1,759.8 億美元(+44.2%)，以資通與
視聽產品增 1,023.7 億美元(+68.5%)、電子零組件增 629.5 億美元
(+45.8%)增加較多。

生產等出現結構性轉變，爰另予併計。

三、進口主要貨品 
（一）與上年同月比較 
由於 AI 產業鏈國際分工與出口衍生需求之帶動，積體電路及資通產
品進口維持強勁，加上輸入物價明顯上漲，8 月總進口較上年同月增
184.5 億美元(+44.3%)，主要變動如下： 
1. 農工原料進口408.4 億美元， 較上年同月增129.4億美元(+46.4%)，
其中電子零組件增 82.5 億美元(+62.6%)、礦產品增 16.9 億美元
(+38.4%)、電腦之零附件增 14.6 億美元(+84.4%)、基本金屬增 4.5
億美元(+21.8%)、塑化原料增 4.0 億美元(+16.2%)。 
2. 資本設備進口 130.6 億美元， 較上年同月增 38.4 億美元(+41.6%)，
其中資通設備增 27.2 億美元(+1.2 倍)、半導體設備增 2.8 億美元
(+7.3%)、交通運輸設備增 2.8 億美元(+1.1 倍)。 
3. 消費品進口 55.7 億美元，較上年同月增 16.0 億美元(+40.5%)，其
中固態非揮發性儲存裝置增12.1 億美元(+3.7 倍)、藥粧用品增 1.2
億美元(+17.4%)、手機增 1.1 億美元(+78.1%)。

四、主要出口國家/地區 
（一）與上年同月比較 
8 月對陸港、日本出口創新高，對美國、東協為單月第 3 高 
1. 對美國出口 236.7 億美元，較上年同月增 40.6 億美元(+20.7%)，
其中資通與視聽產品增 23.0 億美元(+14.6%)、電子零組件增 11.6
億美元(+1.5 倍)、電機產品增 1.7 億美元(+30.1%)。 
2. 對中國大陸與香港出口 211.5 億美元，較上年同月增 59.7 億美元
(+39.3%)，其中電子零組件增 47.4 億美元(+45.6%)、資通與視聽
產品增 6.7 億美元(+42.9%)、 基本金屬及其製品增4.6 億美元(+1.2
倍)。 
3. 對東協出口 151.3 億美元，較上年同月增 44.9 億美元(+42.2%)，
其中電子零組件增 25.3 億美元(+50.3%)、資通與視聽產品增 12.6
億美元(+41.1%)、礦產品增 4.6 億美元(+1.5 倍)。 
4. 對歐洲出口 44.5 億美元，較上年同月增 11.5 億美元(+34.8%)，其
中資通與視聽產品增 6.6 億美元(+57.0%)、電子零組件增 4.0 億美
元(+1.1 倍)，基本金屬及其製品減 1.4 億美元(-31.0%)。 
5. 對日本出口 36.6 億美元，較上年同月增8.9 億美元(+32.0%)，其中
電子零組件增 8.9 億美元(+76.7%)、 化學品增0.5 億美元(+32.3%)，
資通與視聽產品減 0.6 億美元(-13.9%)。 
（二）與上年同期比較 
累計 1 至 8 月對美國出口 1,803.2 億美元，較上年同期增 631.6 億美
元(+53.9%)，對中國大陸與香港出口 1,394.7 億美元，增 307.5 億美
元(+28.3%)， 對東協出口1,150.7 億美元 ， 增372.3 億美元(+47.8%)，
對歐洲出口 376.6 億美元 ， 增130.7 億美元(+53.1%)， 對日本出口247.0
億美元，增 54.7 億美元(+28.4%)。

五、主要進口國家/地區 
（一）與上年同月比較 
8 月自中國大陸與香港進口 126.8 億美元 ， 較上年同月增47.6 億美元
(+60.2%)，自東協進口 106.9 億美元，增46.7 億美元(+77.6%)，自南
韓進口 91.7 億美元，增29.6 億美元(+47.7%)，自日本進口 57.0 億美
元，增 11.2 億美元(+24.4%)，自美國進口 53.9 億美元，增 14.3 億美
元(+35.9%)，自歐洲進口 49.5 億美元，增 6.2 億美元(+14.3%)。 
（二）與上年同期比較 
累計 1 至 8 月自主要國家/地區進口與上年同期比較 ， 自東協增75.4%、
自南韓增 63.2%、自中國大陸與香港增 40.5%、自美國增 24.4%、自
日本增 24.1%、自歐洲增 12.8%。

六、主要國家/地區貿易概況 
（一）今年 1-7 月主要國家/地區外貿景氣普遍上升，南韓(1-8 月)、香港、
新加坡出口動能尤為強勁，分別年增 52.8%、46.6%、38.3%；中國
大陸(1-8 月)、美國出口分別年增 19.3%及 14.6%，德國(1-6 月)、日
本各年增 10.9%、8.1%。 
（二）主要國家/地區進口同步上揚，累計 1-7 月以香港進口年增 48.1%最
為顯著， 新加坡、 中國大陸(1-8 月)分別年增 37.0%、27.0%，南韓(1-8
月)、德國(1-6 月)各增 18.7%、11.7%，日本、美國各增 6.5%、1.2%。

圖 5 2026 年主要國家/地區出進口年增率
七、綜合分析
（一）得益於人工智慧(AI)、高效能運算及 雲端服務等應用 需求強勁， 加
以外銷產品價格攀升，8 月出口 824.0 億美元，創歷年單月新高，年
增 41.0%，連續 34 個月正成長；進口 601.0 億美元，年增 44.3%，
主因 AI 產業鏈國際分工與出口衍生需求擴張 ，及農工原料行情上
漲；出、進口互抵後，出超 223.0 億美元，亦創單月新高 。累計 1
至 8 月出口年增 44.2%、進口增 40.4%。
（二）8 月出口主要貨品以電子零組件、資通與視聽產品貢獻最多，年增率
分別為 58.0%及 41.8%，前者受惠 AI 商機及記憶體外銷價量齊揚，規
模值創單月新高，兩類併計增 49.3%，其餘貨類平均增 16.3%，以礦
產品隨油品售價上漲，年增 70.2%較為突出，基本金屬及其製品、電
機產品各增 22.4%、22.3%。累計 1 至 8 月資通與視聽產品出口值已
超越歷年水準，與電子零組件合計占總出口比重達 78.7%。

（三）8 月對主要市場出口同呈雙位數成長，其中對中國大陸與香港、日本
受電子零組件外銷強勁推升，出口值同創單月新高，各年增 39.3%、
32.0%，對東協、歐洲、美國分別增 42.2%、34.8%、20.7%；五大市
場之外，對墨西哥及澳大利亞出口皆增逾 2 倍。累計 1 至 8 月對美
國、歐洲出口各年增 53.9%、53.1%，對東協年增 47.8%，對日本、
陸港各增 28.4%、28.3%，對五大市場出口規模均為歷年同期最佳。
（四）展望未來，地緣政治衝突 及美國關稅政策變化等不確定 因素，持續
干擾全球經濟成長力道，惟隨人工智慧應用 不斷深化，算力需求攀
升，國際雲端服務業者加速建置 AI 基礎設施，加以國內半導體及伺
服器等供應鏈具競爭優勢，均有助支撐我國外銷動能，下半年出口
可望維持成長態勢。

InvesTips: Annual Wealth Check: Evaluating Your Financial Health | September 2026
Many of us undergo routine yearly health check-ups to identify medical
conditions early, initiate timely treatment and ensure or improve overall
well-being. Similarly, an annual wealth checkup can help maintain monetary
wellness and elevate financial outcomes. Reviewing your money situation once
a year can help you understand whether your income, spending, savings and

investments are still on track. It can also help you make small adjustments
before problems become bigger. For example, research from a 2022 Vanguard
report suggests that reviewing and rebalancing an investment portfolio once
a year can help investors manage risk and capture potential returns, while
keeping transaction costs lower than if they rebalance every month.
Our report serves as a comprehensive guide that can help you assess your
financial health and investment strategies to elevate your monetary outcomes

and achieve sustained financial success.
A comprehensive guide to assessing your
financial health and investment strategies
on an annual basis.
Steve Brice
Global Chief Investment Officer
Manpreet Gill
Chief Investment Officer, EMEA
Hannah Chew
Portfolio Strategist

### Page 3

3
InvesTips: Annual Wealth Check: Evaluating Your Financial Health | September 2026
Importance of wealth checks
Financial health refers to the prevalent economic condition of individuals or
families and their ability to manage their cash flows and investments.
An individual’s financial health takes into consideration their income,
expenses, savings, investments and debt obligations. Ensuring robust and
healthy cash flows promotes long-term stability and resilience. It can also help

avoid unpleasant financial situations such as going over budget or falling into
a debt trap. Some of the reasons to conduct a financial health check annually
are listed below:
Ensure alignment with life changes
A Bankrate (2024) survey asserts that aligning finances with life changes is crucial to mental health.
Around 47% of US adults indicated that money concerns have a negative impact on their mental
health. Each stage of life entails unique financial concerns; for instance, 39% of millennials were anxious

about debt payments and rising costs of living, whereas 30% of Gen Z-ers were concerned about their
student loans and job market conditions. When financial decisions reflect your prevalent life stage and
priorities, there is scope for improved decision-making and resource allocation. Being aware of one’s
financial situation often leads to intentional spending and adaptability. In essence, financial planning
can become a technique to optimise personal fulfilment and not just a means to accumulate wealth.

By aligning finances to prevalent situations and addressing challenges, the financial planning process
can be transformed into a focused life strategy.
T rack financial progress
The financial planning process begins by setting specific goals and aligning money decisions to these
goals. It is important that individuals track their progress against the initial plan to ensure their wealth
journey is on track. Conducting an annual review enables investors to calculate their net worth and gain

a clear overview of their financial position, including their income, savings, investments, debt and needs
for a rainy day. Over the years, this will also provide insight into how wealth has grown and whether
financial habits remain aligned with long-term goals. Evidence suggests that individuals who track
their net worth regularly are likely to achieve their financial goals comfortably and on time. This can be
supported by making timely adjustments across different areas of personal finance, such as spending,

saving, debt management, insurance coverage and investment allocation. Strong financial health
is characterised by consistent growth of wealth with positive returns on investments over time, and
healthy cash flows. Annual reviews can help ensure that one’s broader financial plan remains aligned
with personal goals, life stage and risk tolerance levels. Tracking financial progress also promotes more
informed and confident decision-making.
Consider risk exposures

Vanguard’s 2022 report indicates that, by not rebalancing the portfolio regularly, investors may
inadvertently increase their exposure to high-risk investments disproportionately. The chart below shows
that a portfolio with 60% investment in equities in the year 1990, if left unattended, would have reached
71% exposure in equities by the end of July 2026. Periodic portfolio rebalancing should ensure that the
asset allocation remains aligned to the goals and risk tolerance levels of the individual. In addition, such

reviews help investors adjust their investment strategies to prevalent market trends, interest rates and
economic forecasts.

### Page 4

4
InvesTips: Annual Wealth Check: Evaluating Your Financial Health | September 2026
Capitalise opportunities
Regular reviews allow investors to identify winning opportunities and increase allocation towards
investments that can potentially generate outsized returns. These reviews also enable them to adjust
portfolios to market trends and shift strategies to capitalise on emerging opportunities. For example, in
current markets, companies operating in the AI space look promising, and an investor may benefit from

buying stocks, mutual funds or ETFs that provide the relevant exposure. Many studies have established
that optimal asset allocation contributes to over 90% of a portfolio’s return. Contrarily, only about 7%
may be attributed to stock selection and market timing. Regular reviews can help identify profitable
avenues and adjust allocations to generate better returns.

Financial health check-up process
Although the specifics of the wealth check process may differ based
on the unique needs of the individual, you would need to include a
few key steps to ensure sustained financial success.
Analyse personal goals and priorities: Personal life events
such as marriage, parenthood and a new job immensely
affect financial goals and risk tolerance levels. The first

step in an annual review is to assess the life transitions
that have occurred over the past year, as this will prompt
changes in personal objectives, risk levels and timelines. For
instance, once an individual becomes a parent, they would
need to begin planning for the child’s future education.

6
InvesTips: Annual Wealth Check: Evaluating Your Financial Health | September 2026
obligations. A financial health check should revisit the
assessment to ascertain if circumstances have changed
and accordingly make revisions in the coverage amount
(only if required).
•  Health insurance: As part of the annual check, examine
the past year’s health incidentals and the extent of out-
of-pocket expenses. Evaluate the need for changes to
the existing health policy to ensure enhanced coverage

and reduced self-funding.
•  Property insurance: Any new valuable purchase in the
past year may need to be a part of property insurance
coverage. It is also important to reassess the valuation
of existing property, evaluate any emerging risks and
adequately cover for them. For instance, digital assets
may require cyber insurance coverage to protect against
cyber vulnerabilities.
Examine retirement contributions: Retirement savings
should also be evaluated continuously to ensure the

corpus created will be able to withstand unique risks such
as longevity and policy changes. As part of this exercise,
investors should compare the current retirement corpus
against the projected corpus based on their age and
income. Any deviations (lower actuals versus projections)
should be adequately compensated by increasing
contributions. Individuals should aim to contribute around
15% of gross household income towards retirement savings,
as this level can provide sufficient momentum for long-term

wealth accumulation while preserving flexibility to meet
other important financial goals; this will help them create
a corpus that endures. Leveraging tax-efficient strategies
(tax-advantaged accounts and strategic withdrawals) and
diversifying investments can also help build a sustainable
corpus over time.
Address foundational aspects: The adequacy of
emergency funds should be evaluated, and any shortfall
should be replenished. The review should also ensure that

the emergency funds are aligned to changing personal
and economic circumstances. For example, after the birth
of a child, household expenses would have increased;
therefore, the emergency funds would have to be increased
correspondingly. Although budgets are often drawn
monthly or in line with one’s income flow, spending patterns
and cash flow may need a detailed analysis to assess if the
budget is realistic. This investigation will also help identify

impulse-led expenses and overspending bouts. Awareness
about these patterns fosters accountability and enables
better control over saving and spending objectives.
Check insurance adequacy: One of the most important
elements of financial planning is insurance coverage, as
it ensures a strong foundation and a sense of security.
While insurance may be seen as a one-and-done process,
it requires periodic assessment to align coverage with
changing requirements. Annual revisions of coverage

amount may not be necessary; that said, life events and
other circumstantial changes may require strengthening
the coverage. An annual financial health check should
evaluate the insurance adequacy across life, health,
property and other domains. Some suggestions on
evaluation of insurance coverage are discussed below:
•  Life insurance: Many thumb rules are used to assess
coverage amount; one such rule, for instance, suggests
a cover of 15x one’s annual income. These methods,

though, are quite crude and may not necessarily
provide comprehensive coverage suited to individual
requirements. At the start of one’s financial journey, it
is recommended that individuals conduct a detailed
assessment on the coverage amount and customise
the insurance policy to cater to the family’s needs and

### Page 7

7
InvesTips: Annual Wealth Check: Evaluating Your Financial Health | September 2026
benchmark, and causes for any underperformance are
identified. T ake necessary corrective actions that can
lead to optimal performance of the portfolio in the future.
Investors may also need to evaluate other elements
such as budgeting techniques and decision-making
frameworks against suitable benchmarks. Identify areas
that need improvement, such as excess debt, under saving

or overspending, and make adjustments to improve
outcomes.
Assess tax and cost efficiency: While taxes and costs may
not be on an investor’s mind, actively managing these
elements can increase net returns. ‘T ax alpha’ refers to
the potential value created by efficient tax management
of investments. Strategies to achieve tax alpha include
investing via tax-exempt or tax-deferred accounts and
using strategic withdrawal techniques – for example,
maximising contributions to tax-advantaged accounts.

Efficient tax management can enhance after-tax returns
by deferring and reducing tax liabilities, allowing more
capital to remain invested and compound over time. The
magnitude of benefit, of course, depends significantly on
individual circumstances, including the taxation regime
where you reside, marginal tax rate, portfolio structure and
investment behaviour. Investors can look to implement
a structured cost-benefit analysis to analyse significant

expenditures and investments. This process weighs benefits
against costs and taxes to determine if the investment or
expenditure can elevate continued success.
Update nominations, wills and power of attorney: While
it may not be necessary to update these documents every
year, it is always prudent to go over these aspects. This
will ensure that beneficiary designations are up to date,
nominations and asset distributions align with current
wishes and that the documents are legally valid. Any life

changes always warrant an update of these documents.
If executors, trustees or attorneys have been engaged as
part of this process, it is important to evaluate if they can
continue to act effectively.
Assess credit health: Keeping debt in check is integral to
maintaining strong financial health. Without appropriate
debt management, one can easily fall into debt traps.
Credit health can be assessed using the debt-to-income
(DTI) ratio; typically, lower ratios reflect strong financial

health. DTI lower than 35% enables individuals to manage
their finances comfortably and is considered a healthy ratio
by lenders. It is also important to use credit cards prudently;
they remain one of the leading causes of unmanageable
debt among adults.
Evaluate asset allocation: An investment strategy can be
aligned to market trends, personal objectives and risk levels
after reviewing the asset allocation. T ypically, the current
asset mix is compared against the desired or ideal target

mix, and any significant deviations are adjusted after
analysing market conditions. As part of this effort, it is also
important to reassess risk tolerance levels and adjust asset
allocations as required.
Check diversification: A well-diversified portfolio is
one that aligns with market trends and provides stable
and consistent returns over time. Regularly reviewing
a portfolio can help investors adapt to market shifts
and position their portfolios well to capitalise on new

opportunities while continuing to manage risk. As part of
the diversification evaluation process, it is important to
ensure that the portfolio avoids concentration risk and
aligns with the investor’s goals and risk levels. The process
should also ascertain balanced asset allocation, mitigating
systemic volatilities and optimising risk-adjusted returns.
In evaluating a diversification strategy, it is important to
understand cross-asset correlations and the changes that

have occurred over the past year. Only a significant change
in the underlying relationship between assets should entail
a change in one’s strategy.
Monitor performance: As part of the exercise, it is also
crucial to assess all the money decisions and identify those
practices that lead to better outcomes. The portfolio’s
performance is typically evaluated against a suitable

InvesTips: Annual Wealth Check: Evaluating Your Financial Health | September 2026
Why annual review?
Multiple studies consider annual review of
investments to be ideal as this frequency is a
cost-efficient means to position the portfolio for new
opportunities. Calendar-based rebalancing done
annually works best owing to the following reasons:
Annual reviews enable investors to
harvest high-performing assets
and buy undervalued investments,
thus capturing market gains and

maintaining desired risk levels.
It ensures alignment of portfolio
allocation with risk tolerance levels.
Unattended portfolios tend to drift
from intended allocations, leading to
increased risk exposures.
More frequent reviews, such as monthly ones,
can lead to higher taxes and transaction costs;
by rebalancing annually, investors can harvest
substantial risk premiums against relatively
lower costs and taxes.

### Page 9

9
InvesTips: Annual Wealth Check: Evaluating Your Financial Health | September 2026
An investor’s financial strategy should evolve along with the markets. Often, investors
stick to an established investment approach that worked in the past, without considering
market changes. This can lead to missed growth opportunities and reduced risk-adjusted
returns.
Ignoring market
cues
Emotional and cognitive biases drive almost 90% of investment decisions. These can lead

to irrational decision-making that can produce sub-optimal results. Adopting a structured
framework in decision-making helps keep one’s emotions in check. Similarly, an objective
review of the investor’s financial health to inspect elements within the wealth landscape
and thus gain actionable insights will promote an informed decision process.
Emotional
investing
Investors often tend to hold on to loss-making investments either in the hope that there

could be a miraculous revival (loss aversion bias) or simply because resources have
already been deployed and it no longer costs them anything to hold the asset (sunk cost
fallacy). Both these behaviours weaken the portfolio’s resilience. It is important to identify
underperformers and weed them out before they become a sizeable part of one’s portfolio.
Neglecting poor
performance
T axes command a large portion of our income, yet many of us ignore actively managing

this aspect. This can lead us to lose out on significant savings that could have been
achieved through strategies such as tax-advantaged/tax-deferred income growth
and tax-loss harvesting. Overlooking tax planning can also lead to investors being
underprepared for potential tax law changes.
Ignoring tax
planning
Common pitfalls
Establishing a routine-based wealth check to manage finances may become
mechanical over time, and this can lead to mistakes that could reduce the

benefits of this process. Some common lapses are discussed here:

### Page 10

10
InvesTips: Annual Wealth Check: Evaluating Your Financial Health | September 2026
An annual wealth check will empower investors
to stay in control of their financial future and
foster a sense of security.
Conclusion
Financial health checks serve as progress markers in an investor’s wealth
journey. If investors deviates from their intended route, these checks can steer
them back on track and help maintain and enhance their wealth. Annual
checks are a cost-effective means to identify areas for improvement and make

necessary adjustments to investment strategies for continued financial success.
It is important that this structured review process provides actionable insights
that elevate money outcomes. Such a proactive approach helps investors adapt
to evolving market conditions/life changes and position themselves well to
capitalise on new opportunities and mitigate emerging risks.

Asia week ahead: RBA decision, key
data on China, Japan, Korea, Indonesia
The Reserve Bank of Australia is expected to raise interest rates by 25bp,
while investors will also be closely watching China’s PMI data, activity
and inflation readings from Japan and South Korea, and Indonesia’s
latest CPI report
Asia Research highlights of the week

Xi-Trump summit: Pandas and positive vibes but limited progress
Bank Indonesia holds, but the hiking cycle may not be over
Oil, not El Niño, is driving Singapore inflation
Australia: RBA expected to hike rates by 25bp
We expect the Reserve Bank of Australia RBA to deliver a decisive 25bp rate hike on Tuesday,
reflecting an economy that continues to run hot across multiple fronts. Labour market

### Page 2

THINK Economic and financial analysis
Article | 25 September 2026 2
conditions remain tight, second-quarter GDP growth surprised to the upside, and recent
inflation readings came in stronger than expected. While the housing market shows signs of
cooling, we expect the RBA to emphasise that risks remain tilted to the upside and further
vigilance is required to ensure prices return sustainably to target.
Separately, August CPI data is likely to accelerate further to 4.1% year-on-year, driven

primarily by higher diesel and food prices, alongside persistent underlying core inflation
pressures.
China: PMIs expected to slow a modest recovery
China releases September purchasing managers’ index data on Wednesday. We look for the
National Bureau of Statistics to report a slight recovery in its official manufacturing and non-
manufacturing PMIs to 50.1 and 49.2, respectively. The RatingDog PMI, which has generally
outperformed lately because of its more export-oriented sample, is out the same day.

Industrial profits data is out Monday. Growth in the series has been moderating over the past 3
months, even as overall profits have improved from previous years.
Japan: Activity and inflation data in focus
Japan releases its August services PPI on Monday. Market consensus is for services inflation to
remain unchanged at 3.6% YoY, indicating that price pressures in the sector remain firm. Retail
sales growth is expected to moderate to 3.2% YoY, from 4.0% in July. Sales are forecast to

decline 0.9% month-on-month after the previous month’s 2.4% increase. Industrial production
is expected to rebound 1.4% MoM, lifting annual growth to 7.0% YoY from 3.9%. The quarterly
Tankan survey is expected to show improvement in business sentiment among large
manufacturers. The index is expected to rise to 25 from 22, while the outlook is seen rising to
22 from 17.
Tokyo inflation data will be released on Friday. Market consensus is for headline inflation to

accelerate to 2.5% YoY, from 1.9%, while core prices rise to 2.4%, from 1.8%. This pickup would
suggest that price pressures remain elevated.
South Korea: Trade surplus to widen as inflation eases
South Korea releases August industrial production data on Wednesday. Market consensus
expects industrial production growth to accelerate to 4.5% YoY, from 3.6% in July. Monthly
output is forecast to rise 0.5%. September trade data, out Thursday, is expected to show a

moderation in both export and import growth to 61.2% YoY and 20.7% YoY, respectively.
Despite slower growth, the trade surplus is forecast to widen to $38.4bn, supported by
continued strength in technology and semiconductor exports. Headline CPI inflation is
expected to ease to 3.0% YoY in September, from 3.1% in August. Core inflation is forecast to
slow more sharply to 2.8% YoY, from 3.4%.

Document 213: Capital Market Outlook (2026-09-08)

Report source: Merrill Lynch (CMO)

### Page 1

CHIEF INVESTMENT OFFICE
Capital Market Outlook

### Page 2

2 of 8 September 8, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MACRO STRATEGY
Commodities: From Inflation Hedge to Structural Growth Asset
Anna Snider, Managing Director and Head of Investment Manager Selection
(Due Diligence)
A broader role in portfolios: For decades, commodities occupied a relatively narrow
role in portfolios: protection against inflation when rising prices eroded the value
of traditional assets. That function remains important, but the investment case is

broadening. Commodities now sit at the intersection of geopolitical fragmentation,
supply-chain security, reshoring, inventory rebuilding, and the buildout of AI and digital
infrastructure. Together, these forces are turning parts of the asset class from purely
cyclical exposures into potential long-term growth beneficiaries.
The diversification case is also becoming more relevant. In Q1, when inflation concerns
peaked, the Bloomberg Commodity Index returned close to 25%, outperforming both

Equities and Fixed Income. At the same time, the relationship among major asset
classes has shifted. Equity and Fixed Income correlations have tightened in recent years,
while commodities have moved in the opposite direction: over the trailing five years,
their correlation with both Equities and bonds has been negative. That combination of
inflation sensitivity and differentiated return behavior can make commodities useful
beyond a simple tactical hedge.

AI infrastructure is a physical buildout: AI is often viewed through semiconductors,
software platforms, and hyperscale data centers. Yet the infrastructure required to
deploy AI is extraordinarily commodity intensive. It requires continuous electricity,
generation capacity, transmission lines, substations, transformers, cooling systems,
networking hardware and structural materials. In that sense, AI is not only a computing
revolution. It is also an energy, infrastructure, and critical-materials investment cycle.

Power is the first constraint. Data centers require reliable, around-the-clock electricity.
Natural gas remains a practical source of incremental generation in the U.S., while
nuclear power is emerging as a strategic long-term option for hyperscalers seeking
stable baseload supply. Solar and wind can add capacity and support decarbonization
goals, but grid reliability and storage become more important as power demand rises.
That supports investment in batteries, backup power and grid-scale storage, with lithium

and graphite among the relevant inputs.
The buildout also increases demand for industrial metals. Copper is a clear beneficiary
because it is essential to transmission, transformers, cooling systems and data-center
wiring. Aluminum and iron ore are used in facilities, electrical equipment, transmission
networks and supporting infrastructure. Semiconductor fabrication adds another layer
of demand through industrial gases such as helium and neon, high-purity quartz for

wafers and optics, and gallium and germanium for advanced chips, photonics, and
telecommunications. AI-enabled automation and robotics also depend on permanent
magnets that use rare earth elements, including neodymium-praseodymium and
dysprosium-terbium.
Demand has moved faster than supply: This demand surge resembles earlier cycles
in semiconductors and memory, where expectations pulled spending forward. Metals
and mining companies are already seeing earnings growth and margin expansion,

yet the industry has struggled to keep pace with demand. The imbalance matters
because commodity supply is slow to respond. Production of many strategic materials
is geographically concentrated, permitting timelines are lengthy, recycling capacity is
limited, and substitution can remain years from commercial viability. New mines and
processing facilities can take a decade or more to reach production.
Investment Implications
Commodities may increasingly serve

three portfolio purposes: inflation
protection, diversification from
traditional assets, and participation in
long-term infrastructure and resource-
demand growth, supporting a diversified
allocation across direct commodities
and select producer Equities rather than
a single directional trade.

### Page 3

3 of 8 September 8, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
Governments are also taking a larger role through industrial policy, export controls,
stockpiling and efforts to secure critical supply chains. Restrictions involving gallium,
germanium, graphite and other strategic materials underscore how geopolitics can affect
availability and pricing. As a result, the current cycle may produce greater scarcity and
pricing power than demand growth alone would suggest.

So far, this dynamic has been most visible in industrial metals, but the same forces could
extend into agriculture. Reshoring changes where food and inputs are produced and
processed, while inventory rebuilding can lift demand for fertilizer, fuel, machinery and
storage. Agriculture has not yet experienced the same earnings and pricing impact as
metals, but supply-chain resilience could make it a broader beneficiary over time.
Cyclical forces still matter: The structural thesis does not eliminate near-term

volatility. Energy remains a key transmission mechanism for inflation because oil and
natural gas underpin transportation, manufacturing and industrial production. Geopolitical
disruptions can cascade through supply chains, raising costs and moving both commodity
futures and spot prices. Currency dynamics matter as well. A stronger U.S. dollar can
restrain commodity prices by increasing costs for non-U.S. buyers, while renewed dollar
weakness could support emerging-market demand and reprice dollar-denominated

assets.
A stabilization of geopolitical tensions could create a more constructive cyclical backdrop
through lower energy volatility, improved business confidence and stronger capital
investment. Conversely, renewed conflict can produce sharp and uneven moves across
the complex. These crosscurrents reinforce the value of treating commodities as a
diversified allocation rather than a single directional trade.
Gold, implementation and the portfolio case: Gold is fundamentally different from

most industrial commodities, but it deserves attention. Its traditional relationships with
interest rates and the dollar have been less reliable lately. Even so, sustained central bank
and exchange-traded fund buying points to durable long-term demand despite bouts of
volatility such as the selloff earlier this year. Gold also tended to move inversely with oil
during the height of the Iran conflict, illustrating how different commodities can respond

differently to the same geopolitical shock.
Investors can access the theme through commodity futures or producer Equities. Direct
commodity exposure is generally the clearer vehicle for inflation protection, scarcity, and
geopolitical supply shocks. Producer equities can offer greater upside when commodity
prices rise faster than operating costs because earnings and margins expand, but that
leverage is asymmetric. Company-specific execution, financing, cost and valuation risks

can still cause Equities to underperform the underlying commodity. A blended approach
may capture both physical scarcity and corporate operating leverage.
Ultimately, commodities remain sensitive to energy markets, currencies, growth, and
geopolitics, but their portfolio role is expanding. They can provide inflation protection and
diversification while offering exposure to AI infrastructure, electrification, energy security,
defense modernization, reshoring and, potentially, agricultural supply-chain investment.

The strongest beneficiaries of the AI trade may therefore extend well beyond technology
companies to the materials and power systems that make deployment possible.

### Page 4

4 of 8 September 8, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MARKET VIEW
Rising Global Interest Rates and International Markets
Ehiwario Efeyini, Director and Senior Investment Strategist
Strong earnings growth has so far delivered a fourth consecutive year of double-digit
gains for global Equity indexes in 2026, propelling them to new all-time highs. But a
growing risk throughout the year has been the increase in government bond yields, which

have also broken out to multi-decade highs across a number of the major markets. The
U.S. Treasury will this week begin its expanded Treasury securities buyback program
announced in mid-August in response to rising rates. But 10-year yields across all
the major G10 countries have moved higher, in many cases by as much as or more
than in the U.S. market. Against a backdrop of above-trend growth in nominal gross
domestic product (GDP), high energy prices and elevated inflation, investors appear to

be reassessing monetary policy expectations, particularly in Europe where most of the
2026 increase in 10-year yields has been driven by inflation breakevens (Exhibit 1). Fixed
Income markets are consequently looking for policy rate hikes from the European Central
Bank later this week and potentially from the Bank of Japan later next month.
The rise in global interest rates has also come alongside an expansion in fiscal policy
across the major markets. In Europe, 2026 and 2027 are expected to be the peak

spending years for defense and infrastructure expenditure plans unveiled last year. The
so-called Readiness 2030 package allocates €800 billion to increased European Union
defense spending as it looks to move toward its 5% of GDP target by 2035. And at the
same time, the German government relaxation of its national debt brake has directed
€500 billion toward funding for investment in clean energy, transportation and digital
infrastructure. Similarly in Japan, the government supermajority is looking to enact a

new stimulus aimed at raising investment levels across 17 strategic sectors such as
semiconductors, defense and shipbuilding, in addition to a planned two-year cut to the
consumption tax on food from April 2027. And in the UK, the new central government
leadership is also likely to announce new spending measures when its first national
budget is released next month. To the extent that increased public investment spending
can lift productivity and support nominal GDP growth in Europe and Japan, the rise in

yields need not undermine economic growth or derail the equity market advance. But
higher rates, especially in response to supply-driven energy price inflation, nonetheless
remain a risk for developed market equity returns going into the end of the year.
Exhibit 1: Global Bond Yields in Major Markets have Risen by as Much or More
than U.S. Yields in 2026.

For many emerging markets the picture looks quite different. In contrast with the U.S.,
Japan and Europe, inflation rates are generally well below central bank targets outside
a limited number of resource exporters in Latin America and the booming AI-linked
economies of Korea and Taiwan (Exhibit 2). As a result, policy rate expectations over

the coming quarters are stable or lower for several emerging markets (including China,
Malaysia, Thailand, Poland, Brazil and Mexico) according to Bloomberg consensus forecasts.
Investment Implications
Ongoing strength in economic growth
and corporate earnings should support
global equity markets even in the face
of rising rates. For Emerging Markets
(EM) Equities in particular, contained
inflation, flat to lower local policy rate
expectations and stronger fundamentals

should limit the impact. And we
continue to favor EM Equities over
International Developed Markets, where

target, central banks are expected
to raise rates and investors will be

Historically, rising U.S. interest rates have been a major headwind for emerging markets.
But looking ahead in the current cycle, we still expect the impact of potential Federal
Reserve (Fed) rate hikes to be lower than in the past. Over recent years, the association
between tighter Fed policy and weaker EM returns appears to have diminished. EM
Equities for example were resilient in the face of aggressive U.S. rate increases in 2022
and 2023, broadly keeping pace with the rest of the world. This is in sharp contrast to

prior cycles in which tighter Fed policy has resulted in capital flight, deleveraging and
falling asset values across the emerging world. Particularly during the crisis periods of
the 1980s and 1990s—but also more recently during the 2015 taper tantrum—U.S.
monetary tightening has tended to attract capital away from riskier emerging countries
(particularly those with large current account deficits), reducing support for local asset
prices and exchange rates.

Associated strength in the U.S. dollar has typically been an additional hurdle. For those
emerging economies with fixed exchange rates, a loss of export competitiveness has
further undermined current account positions, forcing central banks to run down their
foreign exchange reserves. And where exchange rates are floating, foreign currency
debts have risen in local currency terms, resulting in widespread defaults, debt
downgrades, recessions and additional exchange rate depreciation.

But starting in the years that followed the 1997-1998 Asian financial crisis and
continuing into the current cycle, the fundamentals across much of the EM universe have
improved. Fixed exchange rates have been abandoned. EM foreign currency reserves have
roughly doubled as a share of GDP . The share of EM debt denominated in U.S. dollars
has fallen significantly, led by the private sector across households and corporates. And
for most countries, current account deficits have narrowed or disappeared entirely. As a

result, emerging markets have become much more insulated from the direction of U.S.
interest rates on a structural basis, and this has only been reinforced by the AI-driven
revaluation of key Asian markets in 2026. Greater concentration in Korea and Taiwan has
increased the EM weighting in the surplus Asia-Pacific region to 83% as at the end of
August—up from 81% at the start of the year and 71% a decade ago.
Above-trend growth in nominal GDP and ongoing strength in corporate earnings should

support global equity markets even in the face of rising interest rates. But for emerging
Equities in particular, contained inflation relative to central bank targets, flat to lower
local policy rate expectations and stronger economic fundamentals should limit the
impact from higher global rates. We continue to favor EM Equities over International
Developed Markets, where inflation levels are generally still above target, central banks
are expected to raise interest rates and investors will be watching for fiscal sustainability

as governments implement expansionary budget plans.

THOUGHT OF THE WEEK
The One-Offs Inflating Q2 Earnings
Lauren Sanfilippo, Director and Senior Investment Strategist
Headline Q2 earnings numbers look almost too good to be true. And in a sense, they are.
With nearly all of S&P 500 companies’ results reported, EPS growth is tracking around
50% year-over-year (YoY)—not just an acceleration from Q1 but a second consecutive
quarter above 20%. Chiefly skewing the headline number are massive mark-to-market

gains on private investments for a handful of mega cap technology companies like
Alphabet and Amazon that booked outsized non-operating gains tied to their stakes in
Anthropic, while Microsoft benefited from its OpenAI investment. Strip those out, and
adjusted EPS growth notches down to 31%. The point being, after being sliced and
diced, 30%+ is well above any long-run historical earnings growth rate.
The one-off nature of earnings strength wasn’t limited to investment gains. A second,

less-discussed boost was tariff refunds. As Exhibit 3 illustrates, revenues from tariffs
steadily climbed from early 2025 through mid-2026 until the International Emergency
Economic Powers Act (IEEPA) tariff ruling which initiated the refund process. As
refunds were disbursed, customs receipts turned net negative in June and July, with
refund payments exceeding total tariff collections. Customs and Border Protection cash
withdrawals of more than $100 billion in refunds have now been paid—60% of the $165

billion in total eligible claims.
The recipients: the “importers of record,” or the dozens of S&P 500 companies that
have disclosed billions in refunds over the past two quarters; Apple alone received $2.19
billion, adding $0.11 to per-share earnings to their quarter. Other disclosed refunds
include Nike with $986 million, Amazon with $640 million, and General Motors refunded
$500 million (although accounting treatment and use of proceeds has varied).

The boost of investment gains and/or refunds may not be repeatable in future quarters.
Perhaps more telling is that the median company’s revenue grew roughly 15% YoY, the
strongest gain in years, corroborating that there is operating momentum rather than
just an accounting mirage for Q2 earnings. Margins, too, as a percent of GDP are at
their highest back to 1950, hovering north of 22%, see Exhibit 4. That this is occurring
amid rising rates and at a time of geopolitical uncertainty/disruption, rather than a post-

recessionary recovery, makes the results that much more impressive.
The Path Forward: Questions remain about the sustainability of these elevated growth
rates, although consensus expects EPS growth to stay above 20% in both Q3 and Q4.
If achieved, every quarter this year would see 20%-plus EPS growth. On the back of
brighter outlooks from Corporate America in recent weeks, full-year earnings growth
will decelerate into the mid-teens in 2027, but that moderation comes off a high base

of 2026. For now, the breadth of earnings growth is the encouraging story as 10 of
the 11 sectors reported growth (Energy and Communication Services leading the way,
Healthcare the only detractor). That’s a reflection of strong core businesses growing
revenues into 2027’s tougher YoY comparisons.
Investment Implications
Underlying earnings-per-share (EPS)
growth of around 30%, stripped
of investment gains, is historically
exceptional. It remains that robust

corporate earnings and positive
revisions have helped Equities absorb
concerns around the recent rise
in yields.

Investments have varying degrees of risk. Some of the risks involved with equity securities include the possibility that the value of the stocks may fluctuate in response to events specific to

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.
Alternative investments are speculative and involve a high degree of risk.

investments, you should consider your overall financial situation, how much money you have to invest, your need for liquidity, and your tolerance for risk.

$6
per gallon for the first time in history, the 10-year U.S. Treasury yield breached 5%, and

this has historically been an attractive entry point. Since 1950, the S&P 500 has never
been
negative in the six or twelve months post-midterm election, boasting average
returns of 13.8% and 15.8%, respectively. Volatility is likely to rise into October before
moderating post-election. A correction amid a strong fundamental economic backdrop
would be a
potential buying opportunity in our view.

MACRO STRATEGY
The Yen, 10-Year Yield and Iran:  Bessent’s Three-Front War and the Stakes for Investors
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
Scott Bessent may carry the title of Treasury Secretary, but these days he looks more like
America’s financial field marshal. His battlefield stretches from Tokyo, where he is trying
to engineer a stronger Japan yen; to the U.S. Treasury market, where he is battling to keep

the 10-year yield from breaking decisively above 5%; and Tehran, where he has become the
point man in the escalating U.S. campaign to squeeze Iran financially and economically.
Make no mistake about it: Secretary Bessent and the Treasury Department are increasingly
an instrument of American economic statecraft, charged not simply with financing the
government and overseeing the financial system, but with managing markets, currencies
and geopolitical adversaries simultaneously. Welcome to the new age of financial warfare.

Front One: Defending the Yen. The first front unfolded over the summer when Secretary
Bessent and the BoJ stealthily intervened in support of the yen, which had fallen to a near
40-year low against the dollar by late July. To arrest the yen’s decline, the U.S. Treasury sold
euros instead of dollars in support of the yen, while pushing the BoJ to make use of the
Fed’s emergency repo facilities. In further support of the yen, the BoJ did what Bessent and

the markets have been pining for, raising its official rate by 25 basis points (bps) to 1.25%.
Since intervening in the foreign exchange markets, the yen has appreciated by nearly 5%
against the U.S. dollar, alleviating concerns of a disorderly unwind of the yen and giving
Bessent—thus far—an important victory. Defending the yen, of course, isn’t simply about
Japan—it’s about the U.S. and the need for foreign capital inflows.
Japan is one of America’s largest foreign creditors. A persistently weak yen creates

pressure on Tokyo to defend its currency, potentially by selling some of its enormous
holdings of foreign assets, including U.S. Treasurys. That prospect didn’t sit well with the
Treasury secretary. At precisely the moment Washington needs foreign capital to finance
its enormous fiscal deficits, the last thing Mr. Bessent wants is one of America’s largest
creditors to become a significant seller of Treasury securities. Hence, the battle to backstop

Japan’s currency.
Front Two: Defending the 10-Year Bond. Mr. Bessent has had less success on the
second front: the U.S. bond market. While the 10-year Treasury yield slipped slightly
following last week’s decision by the Fed to boost the fed funds rate by 25 bps, the
benchmark interest rate is up roughly 85 bps since the start of the year. Stubbornly high
inflation, elevated oil prices, enormous government borrowing requirements and growing
investor anxiety over America’s fiscal trajectory have all converged and conspired to keep

the 10-year yield closer to 5% than 4%.
Field Marshal Bessent is very aware that the 10-year Treasury is the gravitational center of
global finance—and that mortgage rates, corporate borrowing costs, equity valuations and
the government’s own interest bill all take their cue from it. A sustained move materially
above 5% would tighten financial conditions across the American economy, potentially
threaten the Equity bull market, and pile more pressure on the Republican Party.

To prevent this from happening, the Treasury has expanded its bond-buyback operations,
including purchases of longer-dated government securities, in an effort to improve liquidity
and relieve some pressure on the market, while shifting new government debt issuance
into short-term bills. Yet the bond vigilantes have so far proved harder to intimidate than
currency traders. Even after Treasury announced additional purchases, yields continued
climbing before receiving some relief from the latest hawkish move from the Fed.

Over the medium term, if investors believe Washington is borrowing too much, inflation is
running too hot or the supply of bonds is overwhelming global demand, they will demand a
higher yield to own America’s debt. This battle is far from over.
Front Three: Squeezing Tehran. Then there is Iran, where Mr. Bessent is wielding an
entirely different weapon: America’s control over the plumbing of global finance. In practice
Investment Implications
We expect continued investor

support and preference for real hard
assets, and a bias towards a plethora
of commodities and industrial plays.

### Page 3

3 of 8 September 21, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
this means the Treasury can make a foreign bank choose between doing business
with Iran or the U.S. It can freeze assets, blacklist companies, restrict transactions and
pressure allies to close financial escape routes.
Under “Operation Economic Outcast,” billed as economic “D-Day,” Treasury has
intensified sanctions against Iranian entities and financial networks, including recent

actions against Iran aviation and additional efforts aimed at banks and organizations
facilitating Tehran’s international transactions. Mr. Bessent has described the objective
in unusually stark terms: to “economically asphyxiate” the Iran regime. This is economic
warfare in its purest form, but whether it works remains to be seen.
“Asphyxiating” an economy takes time and patience—and international cooperation
and coordination. It means closing multiple global loopholes of third-party entities still

willing and able to do business with Iran—look no further than Russian banks. In addition,
squeezing a major oil producer runs the risk of aggravating global oil supplies, keeping
oil prices elevated. Higher energy costs, in turn, feed right back into the U.S. inflation
outlook the Fed is trying to tame and keeps the bond vigilantes on guard and ready to
push back. Not unlike the battle to suppress U.S. bond yields, the battle to bring Iran to
heel is going to be a long one.

How does it all end? How Scott Bessent’s three-front war plays out remains to be seen. A
decisive victory on all three fronts is unlikely since corralling currency and bond markets
is next to impossible, while the whims of geopolitics can lay waste to the best-laid plans.
That said, however, the U.S. Treasury Secretary has signaled that he is ready to take
a very proactive, pro-growth approach to his job. That’s the good news. The less
encouraging news stems from the inconsistencies in the overarching objectives of the

Treasury secretary.
Mr. Bessent can pressure Iran because access to the dollar-based financial system
remains extraordinarily valuable. He can intervene in foreign-exchange markets because
America’s financial resources give Washington enormous clout. And he can attempt to
influence the Treasury market because U.S. government securities remain the foundation
of the global financial system. But therein lies the contradiction.
The U.S. is simultaneously weaponizing the financial system abroad while becoming more

dependent on that same financial system at home. Washington wants the world to fear
losing access to dollars while also asking the world to lend it trillions of dollars. It wants to
use Treasury and dollar dominance as instruments of geopolitical coercion while running
fiscal deficits that require foreigners to continue believing deeply in those very assets.
The more Washington borrows, the more sensitive it becomes to bond-market discipline.
The more aggressively it weaponizes access to the dollar, the greater the incentive for

adversaries to seek alternatives. And the more the Treasury secretary intervenes directly
in markets, the more investors may begin wondering where economic policy ends and
market management begins.
Exhibit 1: Scott Bessent's Three-Front War.
The Front The Problem Bessent's Objective The Weapon
The Yen Weak yen/currency instability Stronger yen Foreign Exchange intervention, policy coordination, pressure on Japan
The Yield Rising 10-year Treasury yield Lower/contained yields Debt-management strategy, buybacks, issuance policy

Iran Tehran's access to global finance Maximum financial pressure Sanctions, asset restrictions, dollar-system leverage
Source: Chief Investment Office.
For the moment, Mr. Bessent is fighting on three fronts. He wants a stronger yen, a
lower 10-year Treasury yield and an economically isolated Iran. Success on any one front
would be difficult. Achieving all three simultaneously will require considerable skill—and
perhaps a little luck.

4 of 8 September 21, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MARKET VIEW
Beyond AI’s Resource Footprint, Consider its “Handprint”
Anna Potts, CFA, Vice President and Investment Strategist
Since the launch of ChatGPT, investors have celebrated AI’s potential while growing
more concerned about its resource footprint: Behind every AI-generated response is
a physical network of data centers, power plants, transmission lines, cooling systems,

semiconductor facilities and critical-mineral supply chains. No doubt, the AI revolution
has environmental costs. As adoption accelerates, growing demand for power, water,
advanced hardware and raw materials could strain grids, raise emissions and intensify
pressure on local resources. Yet the sustainability conversation should extend beyond
AI’s footprint to consider whether its environmental benefits can outweigh its
resource demands.
AI’s footprint is large, growing and certainly not trivial. AI increases electricity demand,

adds large, concentrated loads to already constrained grids, and may slow the retirement
of polluting energy assets. U.S. data centers could account for 20% of national electricity
consumption by 2035, up from 5.9% today, with power needs reaching 194 gigawatts
(comparable to the electricity demand of more than 150 million homes). 1 Cooling, power
generation and semiconductor manufacturing require water; each new megawatt also
embeds substantial copper, aluminum and other materials; and short hardware cycles

can increase electronic waste. Use varies sharply by workload: Complex reasoning and
agentic tasks can consume many times more energy than a simple text prompt. The
effects are already reaching consumers and communities. Wholesale power costs on
PJM—the largest U.S. grid—rose 76% year-over-year in Q1 2026, while data-center
load included in its previous two capacity auctions added an estimated $13.8 billion to
customer bills. 2 Communities are mobilizing around their concerns, including affordability,

water use and air pollution: Q1 2026 produced the largest single quarterly concentration
of delayed or blocked data-center projects on record. 3 Opposition is therefore becoming
a development constraint to be considered.
Context, caveats and comparisons. Headlines often tell only part of the story. Globally,
data centers account for about 1% of electricity use, 4 modest relative to many industries,
and 9% of estimated global 2025 to 2030 electricity growth. 5 A typical text prompt on

one major company’s AI model 6 uses 0.24 watt-hours of electricity, emits 0.03 grams
of CO₂ and consumes 0.26 milliliters of water—roughly 617,000 prompts match the
emissions from producing and cooking one sirloin steak, while 262,000 match the water
used in an eight-minute shower. 7 Still, billions of interactions, more complex workloads
and growth in resource-constrained regions can create meaningful cumulative impacts.
The most meaningful assessment therefore depends on workload, utilization, location,

grid mix, cooling design, local water availability and who bears the cost of supporting
infrastructure, not one universal number.
AI’s Handprint. While much remains to be seen, AI’s environmental “handprint”—the
benefits it can enable—could potentially prove larger than its direct footprint in some
areas. Across the energy system, AI can help utilities better match electricity supply
and demand, balance grids, optimize storage and reduce wasted energy. Data centers

already account for more than 30% of corporate renewable power-purchase agreements,
helping create long-dated demand for clean generation. 8 Beyond power, AI can improve
precision agriculture, streamline transportation and inventories, and support product

2 BloombergNEF , AI Buildout Drives 76% Power Bill Jump on Largest US Grid, May 14, 2026.

Investment Implications
Responsible AI growth requires
investment in power generation,

grid modernization, energy storage,
advanced cooling, water treatment
and reuse, efficient computing, and
materials recovery and recycling.
Diversified environmental and thematic
strategies may offer exposure to
these physical enablers while reducing
reliance on benchmark names.

design, predictive maintenance, waste sorting and recycling. AI is transforming climate
risk management by using satellite imagery, weather data and predictive analytics to
forecast floods, wildfires and extreme weather events, enabling governments, companies,
and investors to anticipate risks and allocate capital more effectively (Exhibit 2). These
gains are not automatic: they depend on innovation, adoption, and whether efficiency

improvements reduce total resource use rather than simply stimulate more demand. All
industries are faced with the opportunity to responsibly use AI to make operations more
efficient, and much is yet to be discovered.

AI’s potential handprint becomes more tangible in transportation, agriculture and climate
resilience. In aviation, for example, AI can combine weather forecasts, flight paths and
atmospheric conditions to reroute aircraft around regions where warming contrails
are most likely to form—reducing climate impact without requiring a new aircraft or

fuel system. On farms, machine learning can interpret aerial imagery and field-level
sensor data to identify crop stress early, directing water, fertilizer and chemicals to
where they are needed and helping growers improve yields while reducing inputs. AI
can also strengthen climate preparedness by analyzing satellite imagery, weather data
and infrastructure exposure to detect deforestation, anticipate floods or wildfires,
and identify communities, facilities and supply chains most vulnerable to physical

hazards. These are just a few examples that illustrate how AI’s value can extend beyond
incremental efficiency gains to reshape real-world decisions.
Investment Considerations. The challenges created by AI also create potential
investment opportunities. Companies are investing in efficient chips, closed-loop cooling,
reclaimed-water systems, energy storage, grid upgrades and cleaner firm power to
reduce resource intensity and improve reliability. Professionally managed environmental

and thematic strategies can target these “picks and shovels” of the digital buildout,
alongside businesses using AI to improve efficiency across energy management,
water infrastructure, environmental services, waste management and compute. A
disciplined thematic approach can diversify exposure beyond the largest benchmark
constituents while positioning for structural growth in power demand, grid modernization
and resource efficiency. Focusing solely on AI’s footprint risks overlooking both the

companies helping it scale more efficiently and the broader environmental benefits its
handprint may enable.

THOUGHT OF THE WEEK
The Beauty of September
Ariana Chiu, Assistant Vice President and Investment Strategist
Call it an eventful first 13 trading days of September: Brent crude re-eclipsed $100 per
barrel, diesel prices surpassed $6 per gallon for the first time in history, the 10-year U.S.
Treasury yield breached 5%, 9 and the Fed joined the ECB and the BoJ in hiking rates for

the first time since July 2023. Through it all, the S&P 500 has shed 1.4% since the start
of the month, while its equal-weighted counterpart is down by 3.3%.
It’s not all too unexpected that September would be a choppy month for U.S. Equities.
Historical seasonal patterns suggest as much. Since 1928, the average S&P 500 return
in September has come out to -1.1%, 10 not only the worst month of the year on average
but also the only month when the benchmark has ended lower more often than it’s

ended higher. Similar weakness has been seen in midterm election years, with the S&P
500 producing an average September return of -0.8% since 1950. 10
The good news is that this has historically been an attractive entry point for U.S. Equity
investors. Call it the beauty of September: In contrast to September’s average decline,
average returns improve in October (+3.0%), November (+2.7%), and December (+0.8%).
Indeed, the S&P 500 has never been negative in the six or 12 months post-midterm

election since 1950 (Exhibit 4A), boasting an impressive average return of 13.8% and
15.8% over those respective periods. 10
Looking ahead to the coming weeks, we have little reason to believe that 2026
will diverge from the historical trend. Volatility is likely to rise into October before
moderating post-election, in line with what’s typical in the months surrounding the
midterms (Exhibit 4B). A quieter corporate calendar ahead of Q3 earnings means

investor focus will remain on incoming macro data, the path of energy prices, and the
Fed’s policy path.
In the meantime, while the S&P 500 still stands just 2.7% below its mid-August high,
churn beneath the surface already appears to be taking place. Just 38% of S&P 500
members now stand above their 50-day moving average versus 70% a mere month ago. 9
Far from worrisome, a correction in the near term in the context of a strong fundamental
economic backdrop would be a potential buying opportunity in our view.

Economy 
Energy bills: Short-term relief, longer-term challenges 
16 September 2026

Key takeaways 
• Energy bill inflation was around 4% year-over-year (YoY) in August 2026. However, Bank of America internal deposit data shows 
average utility bill payments rose faster, increasing 5% YoY from June through August. This was likely due, in part, to higher 
cooling demand during a warm summer. 
• But bill pressures remain highly uneven across the country. Average utility payments have risen by more than 10% YoY in cities 
such as Detroit, Baltimore and Washington DC, while consumers in Orlando, San Jose and Tampa have seen declines. Some of 
these regional differences appear to reflect policy and infrastructure factors as much as energy usage itself. 
• The long-term outlook still points to upward pressure on electricity bills as demand from data center construction, electrification 
and broader economic activity drive the need for additional generation and grid investment. However, a forecasted strong El 
Niño event this winter could provide some near-term relief, if warmer-than-normal temperatures reduce household heating 
demand, partially offsetting structural cost pressures.

Energy bill inflation has moderated  
Energy is an essential household expense. According to Bureau of Labor Statistics data, energy bills accounted for roughly 3% of 
total household spending as of 2024. US utility prices have been far less affected by higher global oil prices stemming from the 
conflict in the Middle East than gasoline prices, largely because the US is a net exporter of natural gas. Nonetheless, the cost of 
energy has been rising for a prolonged period: since January 2024 the price of electricity has risen 10% and the price of piped 
gas is up 18%.  
The better news is that utility price inflation has moderated relative to the second half of 2025 and early 2026. As of August, 
electricity and piped gas prices were up around 4% year-over-year (YoY). But Bank of America internal deposit data shows that 
average utility payments for electricity and gas were running ahead of inflation in these categories, up 5.3% YoY. One reason 
could be that household demand for cooling was strong this summer, due to relatively warm weather (Exhibit 1).  
Exhibit 1: Utility bill payments rose around 5.3% YoY in August

Energy costs vary widely by region 
While energy bills account for a relatively modest share of household spending nationally, experiences vary across the country. 
Looking at large cities (by Core Based Statistical Areas), our data shows that utility bills rose by more than 10% YoY in Detroit, 
Baltimore and Washington DC from June through August 2026. At the other end of the spectrum, bills fell YoY in Orlando, San 
Joe and Tampa (Exhibit 2).  
Customers’ bills are being pressured by reshoring, reindustrialization, the general pass-through costs in the economy, and a 
significant need for infrastructure replacement in many markets. Regulatory changes and one-off charges have also influenced 
costs in several areas. In Florida, for example, the removal of hurricane recovery surcharges reduced utility bills in 2026, while in 
the Northeast, investments in grid infrastructure and capacity have contributed to higher costs.  
There also remains some disparities in where average bills sit relative to the overall US (Exhibit 3). In Washington DC, Boston, 
Phoenix and Dallas, the average utility bill payment in June through August 2026 was well above the US average. Conversely, 
Chicago, Nashville and Las Vegas paid some of the lowest energy bills relative to the US.

Rising demand for electricity will continue to pressure bills  
Looking longer-term, rising energy demand (particularly for electricity), driven by factors such as data center expansion and 
residential electrification, is likely to keep upward pressure on consumers’ energy bills for a number of years (read Is the AI 
revolution raising consumers' utility bills? and Can the AI buildout keep building? for more).  
Exhibit 4 shows forecasts from the US Energy Information Administration’s August 2026 Short-term Energy Outlook, estimating 
a 4% YoY rise in commercial and industrial electricity consumption. Accommodating this growth will likely require ongoing 
investment in grid capacity and power generation, and some of these costs may be passed on to consumers through higher 
energy bills.  
Exhibit 4: The demand for electricity is rising particularly from commercial and industrial sectors

Short-term, the weather could prove important  
El Niño is the warming phase of ENSO (El Niño–Southern Oscillation), a global climate phenomenon that emerges from variation 
in winds and sea surface temperatures over the Pacific Ocean. El Niño events can have widespread and profound global impacts, 
causing extreme weather such as storms, floods and higher temperatures. The strongest recorded El Niño took place in 1877 to 
1878 and led to a global famine that killed more than 50 million people.  
The National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration (NOAA) believes there is a high probability that a very strong El Niño 
event will occur this year (Exhibit 5). One way to put this in historical context is through the NOAA’s Relative Oceanic Niño Index 
(RONI). A positive RONI indicates the central/eastern tropical Pacific is unusually warm relative to the rest of the tropics. A 
higher RONI indicates a more severe El Niño, though NOAA points out that uncertainty on impacts also tends to rise with RONI.  
Exhibit 5: The NOAA attaches a very high probability to a severe El Niño event over coming months  
Likelihood of an El Nino event estimated by severity (%)

Source: National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration (NOAA)

Exhibit 6 shows that the current forecast for RONI from October to December 2026 is one of the largest on record. In recent 
history, similar-sized El Niño events have only occurred in 1997-98, 2015-16 and to a lesser extent in 2022-23.  
Exhibit 6: The El Niño event in 2026 is forecasted to be the largest on record 
Relative Oceanic Niño Index (RONI) for October-December by year (2026: NOAA 50% probability forecast for October-December 2026)

Source: National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration (NOAA) 
Note: Red bars: 1997-1998/2015-16. Yellow bar: 2026-27 50% forecast. 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
For the US, significant El Niño events tend to bring higher-than-usual temperatures and more rainfall (Exhibit 7). However, no El 
Niño is exactly the same. For example, in 1997-98 California was flooded in but in 2015-16 it was dry, while the nation overall 
was wet. Both time periods were among the highest levels of RONI in the past 70 years.  
Exhibit 7: El Niño events have been noted for higher temperatures and rainfall  
Average winter (December-February) temperatures versus precipitation (data from 1996-97)

### Page 5

I NSTI TUTE

What does this mean for consumers and their energy bills? The most predictable impact from a severe El Niño appears to be 
unseasonably warm temperatures, implying reduced heating needs during the fall and winter. Consumers could therefore receive 
some relief in their utility bills and potentially have additional income to spend on discretionary goods and services over the 
coming months.  
There is a large caveat, however. If El Niño manifests in stormy and rainy conditions – likely impacting the southern parts of the 
US – then some households may end up having to spend on repairs and maintenance following weather damage (read more on 
consumer spending in the aftermath of natural disasters in October’s What's the impact of natural disasters on local 
economies?). This might boost consumer discretionary spending, but not in a way that implies improved well-being. And longer 
term, the broader impact of both climate change and energy demands from the AI revolution are likely to keep upward pressure 
on bills.

Methodology  
Selected Bank of America transaction data is used to inform the macroeconomic views expressed in this report and should be considered in the 
context of other economic indicators and publicly available information. In certain instances, the data may provide directional and/or predictive 
value. The data used is not comprehensive; it is based on aggregated and anonymized selections of Bank of America data and may reflect a 
degree of selection bias and limitations on the data available. 
Any payments data represents aggregated spend from US Retail, Preferred, Small Business and Wealth Management clients with a deposit 
account or credit card. Aggregated spend include total credit card, debit card, ACH, wires, bill pay, business/peer-to-peer, cash, and checks.  
Any Small Business payments data represents aggregate spend from Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit 
card. Payroll payments data include channels such as ACH (automated clearing house), bill pay, checks and wire. Bank of America per Small 
Business client data represents activity spending from active Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit card and 
at least one transaction in each month. Small businesses in this report include business clients within Bank of America and generally defined as 
under $5mm in annual sales revenue.  
Unless otherwise stated, data is not adjusted for seasonality, processing days or portfolio changes, and may be subject to periodic revisions.  
The differences between the total and per household card spending growth rate (if discussed) can be explained by the following reasons:  
1. Overall total card spending growth is partially boosted by the growth in the number of active cardholders in our sample. This could be due to 
an increasing customer base or inactive customers using their cards more frequently. 
2. Per household card spending growth only looks at households that complete at least five transactions with Bank of America cards in the 
month. Per household spending growth isolates impacts from a changing sample size, which could be unrelated to underlying economic 
momentum, and potential spending volatility from less active users. 
3. Overall total card spending includes small business card spending while per household card spending does not. 
4. Differences due to using processing dates (total card spending) versus transaction date (per household card spending). 
5. Other differences including household formations due to young adults moving in and out of their parent’s houses during COVID. 
Any household consumer deposit data based on Bank of America internal data is derived by anonymizing and aggregating data from Bank of 
America consumer deposit accounts in the US and analyzing that data at a highly aggregated level. Whenever median household savings and 
checking balances are quoted, the data is based on a fixed cohort of households that had a consumer deposit account (checking and/or savings 
account) for all months from January 2019 through the most current month of data shown.  
Bank of America aggregated credit/debit card spending per household includes spending from active US households only. Only consumer card 
holders making a minimum of five transactions a month are included in the dataset. Spending from corporate cards are excluded. Data 
regarding merchants who receive payments are identified and classified by the Merchant Categorization Code (MCC) defined by financial 
services companies. The data are mapped using proprietary methods from the MCCs to the North American Industry Classification System 
(NAICS), which is also used by the Census Bureau, in order to classify spending data by subsector. Spending data may also be classified by other 
proprietary methods not using MCCs.  
We consider a measure of services necessity spending that includes but is not limited to groceries, utilities, and gasoline. Discretionary services 
includes but is not limited to charitable donations, leisure travel, entertainment, and professional/consumer services. Discretionary retail 
includes but is not limited to general merchandise, miscellaneous, clothing, electronics and furniture. It excludes categories like groceries and 
gasoline. Holiday spending is defined as items in which spending in the November-December period is usually at least 20% of total annual 
spending on the category.  Value and premium grocers are determined judgmentally on a proprietary analysis of merchants by equity analysts.  
Durables spending is defined as spending on electronics, building materials, auto-related spending and furniture. Premium durables spending is 
based on a selection of retailers who are judged to sell relatively higher value products. Conversely, value durables spending is based on a 
selection of retailers who are judged to sell relatively lower value products.  
Lower, middle and higher household income cuts in Bank of America credit and debit card spending per household, and consumer deposit 
account data are based on quantitative estimates of each households’ income. These quantitative estimates are bucketed according to terciles, 
with a third of households placed in each tercile periodically. The lowest tercile represents ‘lower income’, the middle tercile represents ‘middle 
income’ and the highest tercile ‘higher income’. The income thresholds between these terciles will move over time, reflecting any number of 
factors that impact income, including general wage inflation, changes in social security payments and individual households’ income. The 
income and tercile in which a household is categorised are periodically re-assessed.  
Generations, if discussed, are defined as follows: Gen Z, born after 1995; Younger Millennials: born between 1989-1995; Older Millennials: born 
between 1978-1988; Gen Xers: born between 1965-1977; Baby Boomer: 1946-1964; Traditionalists: pre-1946. 
Any reference to card spending per household on gasoline includes all purchases at gasoline stations and might include purchases of non-gas 
items. 
Additional information about the methodology used to aggregate the data is available upon request.
